- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
3. Q: What is a determinant and which matrices have determinants?
A: Show answer
The determinant is a scalar-valued characteristic of a square matrix. For a matrix A with n rows and n columns, det(A) summarizes properties such as invertibility, orientation, and the scaling of n-dimensional volume under the associated linear mapping. The determinant is not defined for nonsquare matrices in the ordinary square-matrix sense used in the chapter. Although determinants are mathematically fundamental, calculating them explicitly is not the preferred numerical method for many large computational tasks.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the determinant is defined for square matrices and name three properties it helps characterize."
Key point:
- The determinant maps a square matrix to a scalar.
- It is informative about invertibility, volume scaling, and orientation.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
4. Q: How does a determinant determine whether a square matrix is invertible?
A: Show answer
A square matrix A is invertible if and only if det(A) is nonzero. A nonzero determinant means the matrix has full rank and the linear mapping does not collapse the entire space onto a lower-dimensional subspace. If det(A) equals zero, the columns are linearly dependent, the mapping loses at least one dimension, and an inverse does not exist. This equivalence is useful for theoretical classification, although numerical software typically uses more direct algorithms to assess or solve linear systems.
Study instruction:
- "Test the invertibility of a small square matrix by calculating its determinant and relate the result to rank."
Key point:
- A square matrix is invertible exactly when its determinant is nonzero.
- Zero determinant signals rank deficiency and a collapsed dimension.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
5. Q: How is the determinant of a two-by-two matrix calculated?
A: Show answer
For A = [[a,b],[c,d]], the determinant is ad minus bc. The expression compares the two diagonal products and provides a simple measure of the signed area scaling of the mapping in two dimensions. If the result is zero, the two column vectors are linearly dependent and the mapping collapses the plane onto a line or point. If the determinant is nonzero, the columns span the plane and A is invertible. The sign indicates orientation, while the magnitude indicates area scaling.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the determinant of several two-by-two matrices and interpret zero, positive, and negative results."
Key point:
- For a two-by-two matrix, det(A) = ad − bc.
- The value combines invertibility, orientation, and area scaling.

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
6. Q: What is Sarrus' rule and when is it useful?
A: Show answer
Sarrus' rule is a mnemonic for computing the determinant of a three-by-three matrix by adding three diagonal product terms and subtracting the three opposite diagonal product terms. It is limited to three-by-three matrices and does not generalize directly as a convenient rule for larger matrices. The chapter uses it as an explicit small-matrix calculation and compares it with Laplace expansion. For larger matrices, systematic methods such as elimination are more practical.
Study instruction:
- "Apply Sarrus' rule to one three-by-three example and verify the result using another determinant method."
Key point:
- Sarrus' rule is a special calculation aid for three-by-three determinants.
- Do not extend the mnemonic to larger matrices without a valid method.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
7. Q: How does the Laplace expansion compute a determinant?
A: Show answer
The Laplace expansion expresses a determinant as a sum of products of entries from a chosen row or column and the corresponding signed minors. Each minor is the determinant of a smaller matrix obtained by deleting the selected row and column. The alternating signs are determined by the position of each entry. Repeating the expansion reduces the problem to smaller determinants, eventually reaching simple cases. It is useful for understanding determinant structure and hand calculations, but repeated expansion becomes computationally expensive for large matrices.
Study instruction:
- "Expand a small determinant along a row and then along a column, checking that both results agree."
Key point:
- Laplace expansion reduces a determinant to signed smaller determinants.
- It is conceptually useful but usually inefficient for large numerical problems.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
8. Q: What are minors and cofactors in determinant calculations?
A: Show answer
A minor is the determinant of the submatrix formed by removing the row and column associated with a selected element. A cofactor is that minor multiplied by the alternating sign determined by the element's row and column positions. Cofactors appear in the Laplace expansion and in formulas for classical matrix inversion. They help explain how determinant calculations can be recursively reduced. Their definitions should be distinguished because the minor itself does not include the alternating sign.
Study instruction:
- "For a selected element in a three-by-three matrix, identify its minor and then calculate its cofactor."
Key point:
- A minor is a reduced determinant; a cofactor adds the positional sign.
- The sign pattern is essential in Laplace expansion.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
9. Q: Why is the determinant of a triangular matrix the product of its diagonal elements?
A: Show answer
For an upper- or lower-triangular matrix, entries on one side of the diagonal are zero. The determinant therefore simplifies to the product of the diagonal entries. This property makes triangular matrices convenient after Gaussian elimination, which transforms a matrix into an echelon or triangular form while tracking the determinant changes caused by row operations. Once triangular form is reached, the diagonal product gives the determinant without expanding all terms.
Study instruction:
- "Transform a square matrix into triangular form and track row-operation effects before multiplying the diagonal entries."
Key point:
- Triangular form makes determinant calculation a diagonal product.
- Row-operation effects must be tracked so the original determinant is recovered correctly.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
10. Q: How do elementary row or column operations affect a determinant?
A: Show answer
Adding a multiple of one row or column to another leaves the determinant unchanged. Multiplying a row or column by a scalar multiplies the determinant by that scalar. Swapping two rows or columns changes the determinant's sign. These rules explain how elimination can simplify a determinant calculation and why the operations must be tracked. They also help identify when a matrix becomes singular or how determinant magnitude changes under scaling.
Study instruction:
- "Create a determinant calculation using row operations and annotate which operations preserve, scale, or reverse the sign."
Key point:
- Addition operations preserve the determinant; scaling and swapping change it predictably.
- Tracking operation effects is necessary when using elimination to calculate a determinant.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
11. Q: What important properties does the determinant of a matrix product have?
A: Show answer
For square matrices A and B of the same size, det(AB) equals det(A)det(B). The determinant of a transpose equals the determinant of the original matrix, and for an invertible matrix the determinant of its inverse is the reciprocal of the original determinant. Similar matrices share the same determinant. These properties connect determinants with composition of linear mappings, basis changes, and inverse operations. They are useful for proofs and theoretical analysis, but should not be mistaken for a requirement to compute determinants explicitly in every application.
Study instruction:
- "Use determinant identities to simplify a product, transpose, inverse, or similarity expression without expanding it."
Key point:
- Determinants respect matrix products and remain unchanged under transposition and similarity.
- The inverse determinant is the reciprocal when the matrix is invertible.

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
12. Q: What is the geometric interpretation of a determinant?
A: Show answer
The absolute magnitude of a determinant measures how the matrix scales the volume of the parallelepiped formed by its column vectors. In two dimensions, the corresponding object is a parallelogram and the determinant magnitude measures its area; in three dimensions it measures volume. The sign indicates orientation relative to the standard basis. Linearly dependent columns produce a lower-dimensional object with zero volume and therefore a zero determinant. This interpretation makes the determinant more than a symbolic formula.
Study instruction:
- "Draw two or three column vectors and explain how their determinant relates to area or volume and orientation."
Key point:
- Determinant magnitude is volume scaling; its sign indicates orientation.
- Linear dependence collapses volume and produces a zero determinant.

13. Q: Why is explicit determinant computation often avoided for large matrices?
A: Show answer
Determinants are useful for mathematical characterization, but explicit calculation can be costly and numerically unreliable for large matrices. Contemporary numerical methods typically use direct algorithms such as Gaussian elimination, factorization, or specialized routines to solve systems, assess rank, or compute eigenvalues. The chapter emphasizes that determinant calculations by hand are important for understanding, but practical numerical work often avoids using the determinant as the primary computational route. A theoretical identity does not automatically imply an efficient implementation.
Study instruction:
- "For a large linear system, compare the objective of computing a determinant with the objective of solving the system and choose the appropriate numerical approach."
Key point:
- Determinants are theoretical tools; direct numerical algorithms are often better for computation.
- Do not compute a determinant merely to solve a linear system or test numerical stability.

14. Q: What is the trace of a square matrix?
A: Show answer
The trace is the sum of the diagonal elements of a square matrix. It is a scalar characteristic that is linear: the trace of a sum equals the sum of the traces, and scalar multiplication scales the trace. The trace of the identity matrix of size n is n. Unlike the determinant, the trace is simple to calculate directly from the diagonal, and it provides a useful invariant of a linear mapping when its matrix representation changes basis.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the trace of several square matrices and verify linearity using sums and scalar multiples."
Key point:
- Trace is the sum of diagonal entries.
- It is a linear scalar characteristic of a square matrix.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
15. Q: Why is the trace of a matrix product useful?
A: Show answer
The trace satisfies tr(AB) = tr(BA) when the products are dimensionally valid, and more generally it is invariant under cyclic permutations of matrix products. This identity permits algebraic rearrangements that simplify expressions in optimization, probability, and machine learning. For example, the trace of an outer product of two vectors can be rearranged to a scalar inner product. The cyclic property is not the same as arbitrary reordering of factors; the order must remain cyclic and dimensions must be compatible.
Study instruction:
- "Rearrange a trace expression using cyclic invariance and verify that the dimensions of all products are valid."
Key point:
- Trace is invariant under cyclic permutations of a valid matrix product.
- Cyclic rearrangement is not unrestricted commutation of matrices.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
16. Q: Why is the trace invariant under a change of basis?
A: Show answer
If a linear mapping is represented by A in one basis and by a similar matrix S inverse times A times S in another basis, their traces are equal. This follows from the trace's cyclic property. The trace therefore characterizes the linear mapping independently of the particular basis used to represent it. Matrix entries may change under a basis transformation, but the trace remains the same scalar. This is why trace is treated as a matrix invariant rather than merely a sum tied to one coordinate system.
Study instruction:
- "Show that tr(S inverse A S) equals tr(A) using the cyclic property, assuming S is invertible."
Key point:
- Similar matrices have the same trace.
- Trace is a basis-independent characteristic of a linear mapping.
Source links:

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
17. Q: What is the characteristic polynomial of a square matrix?
A: Show answer
The characteristic polynomial is defined from the determinant of A minus lambda times the identity matrix. It is a polynomial in lambda whose roots are the eigenvalues of A. Its coefficients contain matrix characteristics, including the determinant and trace. The polynomial provides a theoretical route to finding eigenvalues, especially for small matrices, although direct polynomial-root calculations are not generally the preferred numerical method for large matrices.
Study instruction:
- "Form the characteristic polynomial for a small square matrix and identify the determinant and trace among its coefficients."
Key point:
- The characteristic polynomial is det(A − lambda I).
- Its roots are the eigenvalues and its coefficients encode matrix characteristics.

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
18. Q: What is an eigenvalue and eigenvector?
A: Show answer
An eigenvalue lambda and nonzero eigenvector x of a square matrix A satisfy Ax = lambda x. The mapping sends the eigenvector to a scalar multiple of itself, so its direction is preserved or reversed while its magnitude changes. Eigenvectors describe special directions of a transformation, and eigenvalues quantify the scaling along those directions. They provide a way to characterize linear mappings and underpin diagonalization, spectral methods, and many machine-learning applications.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a simple matrix, multiply it by a candidate vector, and verify whether the result is a scalar multiple of that vector."
Key point:
- An eigenvector is a nonzero vector whose direction is preserved by the mapping.
- The eigenvalue is the scaling factor associated with that direction.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
19. Q: How are eigenvalues found from the characteristic polynomial?
A: Show answer
Eigenvalues are roots of det(A − lambda I) = 0. The equation requires the shifted matrix to be singular so that a nonzero solution exists to the corresponding homogeneous system. For small matrices, the polynomial can be expanded and its roots found directly. For large matrices, numerical algorithms compute eigenvalues more robustly than explicit characteristic-polynomial expansion. The theoretical definition is useful for understanding, but practical computation must consider numerical conditioning and matrix size.
Study instruction:
- "Derive the characteristic polynomial of a small matrix and solve for its roots, then explain the numerical limitations of the method."
Key point:
- Eigenvalues are roots of the characteristic polynomial.
- Numerical eigensolvers are generally preferable for large matrices.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
20. Q: What is the geometric meaning of an eigenvector?
A: Show answer
An eigenvector points along a direction that the linear mapping does not rotate away from itself. The eigenvalue determines the scaling along that direction; a positive value preserves orientation, a negative value reverses it, and zero collapses the direction. Eigenvectors can therefore reveal the axes along which a transformation stretches, compresses, or removes dimensions. The geometric interpretation is most straightforward for real eigenvalues and real eigenvectors, while rotations in real spaces may involve complex eigenvalues without real eigenvector directions.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a linear mapping and describe how eigenvectors identify directions that are stretched, compressed, reversed, or collapsed."
Key point:
- Eigenvectors identify invariant directions of a mapping.
- Eigenvalues describe scaling and possible direction reversal or collapse.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
21. Q: What is an eigenspace and why is it useful?
A: Show answer
An eigenspace associated with eigenvalue lambda is the solution space of the homogeneous system (A − lambda I)x = 0. It contains all eigenvectors associated with lambda together with the zero vector. Its dimension is the geometric multiplicity of the eigenvalue. Eigenspaces are useful because they organize eigenvectors into subspaces and reveal whether a matrix has enough independent eigenvectors to form a basis for diagonalization.
Study instruction:
- "Solve the homogeneous system for one eigenvalue and describe the resulting eigenspace and its dimension."
Key point:
- An eigenspace is the null space of A − lambda I.
- Its dimension measures the number of independent eigenvector directions for that eigenvalue.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
22. Q: What is the difference between algebraic and geometric multiplicity?
A: Show answer
Algebraic multiplicity is the number of times an eigenvalue appears as a root of the characteristic polynomial. Geometric multiplicity is the dimension of its eigenspace, or the number of linearly independent eigenvectors associated with it. Geometric multiplicity is at least one and cannot exceed algebraic multiplicity. A repeated eigenvalue may have an eigenspace with fewer dimensions than its algebraic multiplicity, which can prevent the matrix from being diagonalizable.
Study instruction:
- "For a repeated eigenvalue, calculate its algebraic and geometric multiplicities and compare them."
Key point:
- Algebraic multiplicity counts polynomial roots; geometric multiplicity counts independent eigenvectors.
- Geometric multiplicity cannot exceed algebraic multiplicity.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
23. Q: What makes a matrix defective?
A: Show answer
A square matrix is defective when it has fewer than n linearly independent eigenvectors, where n is its dimension. A defective matrix cannot be diagonalized because its eigenvectors do not form a basis. It typically has at least one repeated eigenvalue whose geometric multiplicity is smaller than its algebraic multiplicity. Defectiveness is distinct from singularity: a matrix may be invertible yet defective, and a matrix may be singular yet diagonalizable.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a matrix with a repeated eigenvalue and determine whether its eigenspaces provide a complete basis."
Key point:
- Defective matrices lack a full basis of eigenvectors.
- Defectiveness and invertibility are separate properties.

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
24. Q: Why do eigenvectors associated with distinct eigenvalues form a linearly independent set?
A: Show answer
Eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent. This theorem provides a sufficient condition for a square matrix to have a complete eigenvector basis when it has n distinct eigenvalues. The matrix can then be diagonalized. The theorem does not require that every eigenvalue be distinct for diagonalization; repeated eigenvalues are allowed if their eigenspaces collectively provide n independent eigenvectors.
Study instruction:
- "Use the distinct-eigenvalue theorem to explain why a matrix with n distinct eigenvalues has an eigenvector basis."
Key point:
- Distinct eigenvalues guarantee independent eigenvectors.
- Repeated eigenvalues do not automatically prevent diagonalization.

25. Q: What does the spectral theorem say about symmetric matrices?
A: Show answer
A real symmetric matrix has real eigenvalues and an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors. It can therefore be diagonalized using an orthogonal matrix, so its decomposition can be written with the transpose of the orthogonal eigenvector matrix as the inverse. This result is stronger than ordinary diagonalization because it guarantees orthogonality and real eigenvalues. It is important in covariance analysis, spectral methods, and matrix factorization.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a symmetric matrix can be diagonalized orthogonally and identify the roles of the eigenvectors and eigenvalues."
Key point:
- Real symmetric matrices have real eigenvalues and an orthonormal eigenbasis.
- Orthogonal diagonalization simplifies coordinate changes and matrix operations.

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
26. Q: Why is A transpose times A symmetric and positive semidefinite?
A: Show answer
For any real matrix A, the matrix A transpose times A is symmetric because transposing the product returns the same matrix. It is positive semidefinite because for any vector x, the quadratic form x transpose A transpose A x equals the squared norm of Ax, which cannot be negative. If A has full column rank, the quadratic form is strictly positive for nonzero x and the matrix is positive definite. This construction is fundamental to the SVD.
Study instruction:
- "Prove symmetry and nonnegative quadratic form for A transpose A using a vector x."
Key point:
- A transpose A is always symmetric and positive semidefinite.
- Full column rank makes it positive definite.

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
27. Q: How are determinant and eigenvalues related?
A: Show answer
The determinant of a square matrix equals the product of its eigenvalues, counting multiplicity. The eigenvalues may be complex for a real matrix, but their product still yields the determinant. This relationship provides a useful theoretical identity and connects invertibility with eigenvalues: a matrix is singular when at least one eigenvalue is zero. It also explains why the determinant captures the overall scaling associated with eigenvalue directions in cases where an eigenbasis exists.
Study instruction:
- "For a small matrix, calculate eigenvalues and verify that their product equals the determinant."
Key point:
- The determinant is the product of eigenvalues.
- A zero eigenvalue implies a zero determinant and singularity.

28. Q: How are trace and eigenvalues related?
A: Show answer
The trace of a square matrix equals the sum of its eigenvalues, counting multiplicity. This identity is valid even when eigenvalues are complex. It provides a consistency check for eigenvalue calculations and connects the diagonal sum to the spectrum of the matrix. Together, trace and determinant summarize two aspects of the eigenvalues: their sum and their product.
Study instruction:
- "Verify the trace-eigenvalue identity on a small matrix and use it as a consistency check."
Key point:
- Trace equals the sum of eigenvalues.
- Trace and determinant connect matrix entries with spectral characteristics.

A: Show answer
PageRank models the importance of webpages using a directed network and a transition matrix. The limiting importance vector is described as an eigenvector associated with eigenvalue one. The vector is normalized so its entries can be interpreted as relative probabilities or weights. The example shows how eigenvectors can represent a meaningful structure in data rather than merely being an abstract algebraic object. The model's practical details require additional assumptions, but the chapter uses it to illustrate the connection between spectral analysis and network ranking.

30. Q: Why does the chapter symmetrize the C. elegans connectivity matrix?
A: Show answer
The neural connectivity matrix is directed and therefore not symmetric. Its eigenvalues may be complex. The chapter constructs a symmetrized matrix by adding the adjacency matrix to its transpose, which represents connections without regard to direction. This produces a symmetric matrix suitable for real-valued spectral analysis. The symmetrization changes the original data representation, so the interpretation must be limited to the undirected connection structure being analyzed rather than the original directional network.

31. Q: What is the Cholesky decomposition?
A: Show answer
Cholesky decomposition factors a symmetric positive-definite matrix A into L times L transpose, where L is lower triangular with positive diagonal entries. It is a square-root-like matrix factorization. The factor is unique under the stated positive-diagonal convention. The decomposition is useful for numerical computations involving covariance matrices, determinants, and transformations of random variables. It is not guaranteed for every matrix; the symmetry and positive-definiteness conditions matter.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the conditions required for Cholesky factorization and verify them before attempting the decomposition."
Key point:
- Cholesky factorization is A = L L transpose for symmetric positive-definite A.
- The lower-triangular factor with positive diagonal is unique.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
32. Q: Why does Cholesky decomposition require positive definiteness?
A: Show answer
Positive definiteness ensures that the quadratic form associated with A is positive for every nonzero vector, which supports the existence of the Cholesky factor with positive diagonal entries. A symmetric matrix that is merely semidefinite may have zero eigenvalues and may require different handling or fail the standard Cholesky conditions. The decomposition is therefore not a universal factorization. Checking the matrix's symmetry and definiteness prevents invalid assumptions and numerical errors.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish positive-definite from positive-semidefinite matrices and explain why the standard Cholesky factorization is guaranteed for the former."
Key point:
- Positive definiteness is a required condition for the standard Cholesky theorem.
- Semidefinite or nonsymmetric matrices require different treatment.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
33. Q: How is a Cholesky factor constructed?
A: Show answer
The lower-triangular factor is computed progressively. Diagonal entries are obtained from square roots of the original diagonal entries after subtracting contributions from previously calculated factor entries. Off-diagonal entries are obtained by dividing the adjusted matrix entry by the corresponding diagonal factor. Each step depends on earlier calculations, so the process can be implemented efficiently and systematically. The chapter illustrates this pattern for a three-by-three symmetric positive-definite matrix.
Study instruction:
- "Work through a three-by-three Cholesky factorization and show how each factor entry depends on previously calculated entries."
Key point:
- Cholesky construction is recursive across the lower triangle.
- Positive diagonal values and valid square roots depend on positive definiteness.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
34. Q: Why is Cholesky decomposition useful for covariance matrices?
A: Show answer
Covariance matrices are symmetric and, under suitable conditions, positive definite. Cholesky factorization can be used to transform standard random variables into correlated variables with a desired covariance structure. It also supports efficient computations for multivariate Gaussian models, determinants, and numerical operations. The chapter connects this factorization to stochastic models and variational autoencoders. The factorization supports computational implementation but does not itself determine the statistical model or its assumptions.
Study instruction:

- Cholesky factors support covariance-based transformations and sampling.
- The statistical assumptions remain separate from the algebraic factorization.

35. Q: What is a diagonal matrix and why is it computationally convenient?
A: Show answer
A diagonal matrix has zero entries outside its main diagonal. Its determinant is the product of its diagonal entries, its inverse is obtained by reciprocating nonzero diagonal entries, and its powers are calculated by raising each diagonal entry to the corresponding power. These operations are simple because the diagonal structure prevents interactions between coordinates. Diagonal matrices appear naturally in decompositions because they isolate scaling factors or eigenvalues.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the determinant, inverse, and power of a diagonal matrix and explain why each operation is simple."
Key point:
- Diagonal matrices isolate coordinate-wise operations.
- Diagonal structure makes several matrix operations efficient.

36. Q: What does it mean for a matrix to be diagonalizable?
A: Show answer
A square matrix is diagonalizable if it is similar to a diagonal matrix. This means there is an invertible matrix P whose columns form a basis of eigenvectors and a diagonal matrix D containing the corresponding eigenvalues, such that A equals P D P inverse. The decomposition expresses the same linear mapping in an eigenvector basis. Diagonalization requires a complete basis of independent eigenvectors, not merely the existence of eigenvalues.
Study instruction:
- "Determine whether a matrix has enough independent eigenvectors to form P and explain the diagonalization condition."
Key point:
- Diagonalization requires an eigenvector basis.
- The diagonal matrix represents the mapping in that basis.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
37. Q: How does the eigendecomposition A = P D P inverse work?
A: Show answer
The columns of P are eigenvectors of A, while the diagonal entries of D are their corresponding eigenvalues. The factor P inverse changes coordinates from the original basis to the eigenvector basis, D scales each eigenvector coordinate, and P maps the result back. The decomposition is valid when P is invertible, which requires n independent eigenvectors. It is a representation of the same linear mapping rather than a change in the underlying mapping itself.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a vector through the three stages of P inverse, D, and P and explain each stage in the original coordinate system."
Key point:
- P contains the eigenbasis; D contains eigenvalue scaling.
- The decomposition is a sequence of basis change, scaling, and return to the original basis.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
38. Q: When can a symmetric matrix be orthogonally diagonalized?
A: Show answer
Every real symmetric square matrix can be orthogonally diagonalized. Its eigenvectors can be chosen as an orthonormal basis, so the diagonalization uses an orthogonal matrix P and P transpose as its inverse. The diagonal entries are real eigenvalues. This property simplifies geometric interpretation and numerical calculations because orthogonal transformations preserve lengths and inner products.
Study instruction:
- "Compare ordinary diagonalization with orthogonal diagonalization and identify the extra guarantee supplied by symmetry."
Key point:
- Symmetry guarantees orthogonal diagonalization over the real numbers.
- Orthogonal basis changes preserve inner products and norms.

39. Q: How does diagonalization simplify matrix powers?
A: Show answer
If A is diagonalizable as P D P inverse, then its kth power is P D to the kth power P inverse. The diagonal power is easy to compute because each diagonal entry is raised independently. This can make repeated multiplication much more efficient and provides insight into how eigenvalues govern repeated application of the mapping. The method is available only when the required diagonalization exists and should be evaluated for numerical stability in actual computation.
Study instruction:

- Diagonalization converts repeated matrix powers into diagonal powers.

40. Q: How can diagonalization simplify a determinant?
A: Show answer
For a diagonalizable matrix A = P D P inverse, determinant identities reduce the determinant of A to the determinant of D because the determinants of P and P inverse cancel. The determinant of D is the product of its diagonal entries, which are the eigenvalues. This provides an efficient theoretical derivation of the determinant-eigenvalue relationship and a useful consistency check.
Study instruction:
- "Use determinant multiplicativity to derive det(A) from its diagonalization."

- The determinant of a diagonalizable matrix equals the determinant of its diagonal factor.

41. Q: Why is diagonalization not available for every square matrix?
A: Show answer
A square matrix may have fewer than n independent eigenvectors, making it defective. Such a matrix cannot be represented by a diagonal matrix through similarity. Repeated eigenvalues are a common situation where the number of independent eigenvectors must be checked. A matrix can be invertible and still be defective, and some defective matrices have alternative forms such as Jordan normal form, which is beyond the chapter's scope.

42. Q: What is the singular value decomposition?
A: Show answer
The SVD expresses any real matrix A as U Sigma V transpose. U and V are orthogonal matrices and Sigma is a rectangular diagonal-structured matrix containing nonnegative singular values. Unlike eigendecomposition, SVD applies to rectangular matrices and always exists. It provides a structured representation of a mapping between potentially different-dimensional spaces and is fundamental to matrix approximation, dimensionality reduction, data compression, and many numerical methods.
Study instruction:
- "Write the SVD factors and identify their dimensions for a rectangular matrix."
Key point:
- SVD exists for every matrix and uses orthogonal factors.
- The singular values are nonnegative and stored in Sigma.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
43. Q: What is the full SVD and how are its factors dimensioned?
A: Show answer
For A with m rows and n columns, the full SVD uses U of size m by m, Sigma of size m by n, and V of size n by n, so A equals U Sigma V transpose. U and V are orthogonal. Sigma has a diagonal structure and may contain zero padding depending on the dimensions and rank. This full formulation keeps both singular-vector matrices square, although reduced and compact forms are often more economical in implementation.
Study instruction:
- "For a rectangular matrix, write the dimensions of U, Sigma, and V in the full SVD and confirm that the product dimensions match A."
Key point:
- The full SVD uses square orthogonal U and V.
- Sigma has the same dimensions as A.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
44. Q: What are singular values?
A: Show answer
Singular values are the nonnegative diagonal entries of Sigma in the SVD, conventionally ordered from largest to smallest. They quantify scaling along the corresponding singular-vector directions. The nonzero singular values are the square roots of the nonzero eigenvalues of A transpose A and of A A transpose. The number of nonzero singular values equals the matrix rank. Their magnitudes help identify dominant directions and how much structure can be retained in a low-rank approximation.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how singular values relate to rank, scaling, and the eigenvalues of A transpose A."
Key point:
- Singular values are nonnegative and ordered by convention.
- Their count and magnitudes reveal rank and dominant structure.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
45. Q: How are right-singular vectors related to A transpose A?
A: Show answer
The right-singular vectors of A are orthonormal eigenvectors of A transpose A. Because A transpose A is symmetric positive semidefinite, it can be orthogonally diagonalized. Its eigenvalues are the squared singular values. This relationship provides a theoretical route to constructing the SVD, although explicitly forming A transpose A can be numerically undesirable for some computations.
Study instruction:
- "Derive the relation between the SVD and the eigendecomposition of A transpose A and identify the right-singular vectors."
Key point:
- Right-singular vectors diagonalize A transpose A.
- The eigenvalues of A transpose A are squared singular values.

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
46. Q: How are left-singular vectors related to A A transpose?
A: Show answer
The left-singular vectors are orthonormal eigenvectors of A A transpose. This symmetric positive-semidefinite matrix captures the mapping's structure in the codomain. Its nonzero eigenvalues match those of A transpose A, and both sets are squared singular values. The left-singular vectors therefore describe dominant directions in the output space, while the right-singular vectors describe corresponding directions in the input space.
Study instruction:
- "Compare A transpose A and A A transpose and explain which singular-vector set each matrix supplies."
Key point:
- Left-singular vectors diagonalize A A transpose.
- Both Gram matrices share the nonzero squared singular values.

47. Q: What is the singular-value equation?
A: Show answer
The singular-value equation links a right-singular vector v to its corresponding left-singular vector u through A v = sigma u. The vectors belong to different spaces when A is rectangular, so the equation differs from the eigenvalue equation, where the same vector space is involved. The equation is valid for the nonzero singular values and associated vectors, and it provides a direct interpretation of how A maps input directions to output directions with scaling.
Study instruction:
- "Verify the singular-value equation for one pair of singular vectors and explain why u and v need not be the same vector."
Key point:
- A v equals sigma u connects input and output singular directions.
- SVD handles mappings between different-dimensional spaces.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
48. Q: What is the geometric interpretation of the SVD?
A: Show answer
The SVD decomposes a mapping into three stages: a basis change in the domain using V transpose, scaling and dimensional mapping through Sigma, and a basis change in the codomain using U. The singular values determine the scaling along orthogonal directions. This separates the mapping's geometry into rotations or orthogonal transformations and coordinate-wise scaling. It is useful for understanding how a matrix transforms a sphere or grid into an ellipsoid or lower-dimensional shape.
Study instruction:
- "Draw or describe the three stages of an SVD and identify where rotation-like changes and scaling occur."
Key point:
- SVD separates domain basis change, scaling, and codomain basis change.
- Singular values describe the principal scaling of the mapping.

-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
49. Q: How does SVD differ from eigendecomposition?
A: Show answer
Eigendecomposition is defined for square matrices and requires a complete eigenvector basis to diagonalize the matrix. SVD applies to every matrix, including rectangular ones, and its left- and right-singular vectors are orthonormal. Eigenvalues may be negative or complex, while singular values are real and nonnegative. For symmetric matrices, the eigenvalue decomposition and SVD coincide in an appropriate form. SVD is therefore more general and often more robust for data matrices.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the domain, existence conditions, factor dimensions, and scalar factors of eigendecomposition and SVD."
Key point:
- Eigendecomposition is square-matrix and eigenbasis dependent; SVD is universal.
- SVD uses orthogonal factors and nonnegative singular values.

50. Q: What is the difference between full, reduced, and compact SVD?
A: Show answer
The full SVD uses square U and V with a rectangular Sigma. Reduced or thin formulations retain only the dimensions needed to represent the nonzero or relevant singular directions, making storage and computation smaller. Compact forms may use rank-sized factors and a rank-sized diagonal matrix. The mathematical mapping remains the same, but the dimensions and zero-padding conventions differ. The chapter emphasizes the full form for notation and introduces alternative conventions used in practice.
Study instruction:
- "Compare full and reduced SVD dimensions for a tall and a wide matrix."
Key point:
- Different SVD conventions change factor dimensions, not the underlying decomposition.
- Reduced forms avoid storing unnecessary orthogonal directions and zero padding.

51. Q: How can a matrix be represented as a sum of rank-one matrices?
A: Show answer
Using the SVD, a matrix can be expressed as a sum of outer products of left- and right-singular vectors weighted by their singular values. Each term is a rank-one matrix. Summing all nonzero terms reconstructs the original matrix. This representation isolates individual components and supports low-rank approximation, interpretation of dominant patterns, and compression. The outer product creates a matrix from a column vector and a row vector, with rank at most one.
Study instruction:
- "Construct an outer product from two vectors and explain why its rank is at most one."
Key point:
- SVD expresses a matrix as weighted rank-one components.
- The outer product is a building block for matrix approximation.

52. Q: What is a rank-k approximation of a matrix?
A: Show answer
A rank-k approximation retains the first k singular-value components of the SVD and discards the remaining components. It produces a matrix whose rank is at most k, often exactly k when the retained singular values are nonzero. The approximation reduces complexity and can preserve the dominant structure of the original matrix. Its usefulness depends on whether the discarded components represent less important variation, noise, or detail for the intended task.
Study instruction:
- "Form a rank-k approximation from an SVD and identify which singular components are retained and discarded."
Key point:
- Rank-k approximation keeps the k largest singular-value components.
- The choice of k controls the trade-off between representation size and fidelity.

- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
53. Q: What is the spectral norm of a matrix?
A: Show answer
The spectral norm is the maximum ratio of the Euclidean norm of Ax to the Euclidean norm of x over nonzero vectors x. It measures the greatest stretching factor that A can apply to a vector. The chapter shows that the spectral norm equals the largest singular value. This connection makes the norm easy to interpret through the SVD and provides a measure for approximation error and stability.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the spectral norm as maximum stretching and verify its equality with the largest singular value."
Key point:
- The spectral norm measures maximum vector stretching.
- It equals the largest singular value.

54. Q: What does the Eckart–Young theorem establish?
A: Show answer
The Eckart–Young theorem states that the truncated SVD gives an optimal rank-k approximation in the spectral norm. Among matrices of rank k, the SVD-based approximation minimizes the spectral-norm error. The error equals the next singular value after the retained k components. This gives a principled basis for approximation rather than selecting arbitrary low-rank factors. The theorem's optimality is tied to the stated norm and rank constraint, not to every possible metric or task.
Study instruction:
- "State the rank constraint and norm in the Eckart–Young theorem and identify the error associated with a rank-k approximation."
Key point:
- Truncated SVD is optimal for rank-k approximation in the spectral norm.
- The error is governed by the next discarded singular value.

An image can be represented as a matrix of pixel values. The SVD decomposes it into weighted rank-one components, and a low-rank approximation retains only the dominant components. This can reduce storage substantially while preserving recognizable structure. The chapter illustrates how successive rank approximations recover more detail in a Stonehenge image. Compression is lossy because discarded components are not retained, and the visual quality depends on the image and chosen rank.

57. Q: What is the relationship between SVD and dimensionality reduction?
A: Show answer
SVD identifies orthogonal directions ranked by singular-value magnitude. Retaining only the leading directions reduces the number of dimensions used to represent the data. This can preserve dominant structure while reducing storage and computational cost. In applications such as principal component analysis, the relationship to centered data and covariance matters, so preprocessing and the data model must be specified. The chapter connects low-rank approximation to PCA and other spectral methods.

58. Q: Why can forming A transpose A be numerically problematic when computing SVD?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows the mathematical relation between SVD and the eigenvalue decomposition of A transpose A, but it notes that computing SVD through this route can have poor numerical behavior. Forming the Gram matrix can amplify conditioning issues, particularly when singular values vary greatly in magnitude. Numerical software typically uses dedicated SVD algorithms rather than relying on an explicit eigendecomposition of A transpose A. The theoretical construction explains the relationships, while the implementation should prioritize stable algorithms.

59. Q: What is the difference between singularity and defectiveness?
A: Show answer
Singularity means a square matrix is not invertible and has zero determinant, which is equivalent to rank deficiency. Defectiveness means the matrix lacks a complete basis of eigenvectors and cannot be diagonalized. These properties are independent: a matrix may be singular and diagonalizable, or invertible and defective. The matrix taxonomy in the chapter emphasizes that invertibility and diagonalizability classify different structural properties.

60. Q: What is a normal matrix?
A: Show answer
A real square matrix is normal when A transpose A equals A A transpose. Normal matrices include symmetric matrices and orthogonal matrices, but the category is broader. The condition describes a relationship between the matrix and its transpose and supports special spectral properties. The chapter uses normality as part of its matrix taxonomy to distinguish matrix classes and the operations or decompositions available to them.

61. Q: What distinguishes an orthogonal matrix from a symmetric matrix?
A: Show answer
An orthogonal matrix has transpose equal to its inverse and preserves inner products and Euclidean norms. A symmetric matrix equals its transpose. A matrix can satisfy one property without the other. Orthogonal matrices represent norm-preserving transformations such as rotations or reflections, while symmetric matrices have real eigenvalues and can be orthogonally diagonalized. Both classes are important but serve different structural purposes.
Study instruction:
- "Test symmetry and orthogonality separately for a matrix and explain the geometric meaning of each property."
Key point:
- Symmetry concerns equality with the transpose; orthogonality concerns inverse and norm preservation.
- The two properties are not interchangeable.

62. Q: How does positive definiteness relate to eigenvalues and invertibility?
A: Show answer
A real symmetric positive-definite matrix has strictly positive quadratic forms for nonzero vectors and strictly positive eigenvalues. It is therefore invertible and has a nonzero determinant. Positive semidefinite matrices allow zero eigenvalues and may be singular. These distinctions matter for Cholesky factorization, covariance matrices, and spectral analysis. Positive definiteness is a stronger property than symmetry or invertibility alone.
Study instruction:
- "Compare positive-definite and positive-semidefinite matrices through their eigenvalues and quadratic forms."
Key point:
- Positive-definite symmetric matrices have positive eigenvalues and are invertible.
- Positive semidefinite matrices may have zero eigenvalues and be singular.

63. Q: What is the matrix taxonomy presented in Chapter 4?
A: Show answer
The chapter organizes matrix classes according to properties and available operations. Real matrices may be square or nonsquare; square matrices may be singular or regular, defective or diagonalizable, normal or non-normal, symmetric, positive definite, diagonal, or identity. Each class has different consequences for determinants, inverses, eigenvalues, Cholesky factorization, and orthogonal transformations. The taxonomy helps identify which mathematical tools are available before attempting a computation.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a matrix classification tree and annotate each class with a defining property and one available operation or decomposition."
Key point:
- Matrix classes are defined by distinct structural properties.
- Classification guides the choice of mathematical operations and decompositions.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
64. Q: How should matrix decomposition methods be selected for a practical task?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying the matrix's dimensions, rank, symmetry, definiteness, and numerical properties. Determinants and traces support characterization; eigendecomposition is useful for square matrices with an eigenbasis; Cholesky applies to symmetric positive-definite matrices; and SVD applies universally and supports rank approximation. The computational objective also matters: solving a system, compressing data, estimating dominant directions, or generating samples may call for different algorithms. Choose a method that meets the mathematical conditions and numerical needs.
Study instruction:
- "Create a decision table mapping matrix properties and computational objectives to an appropriate method."
Key point:
- Method selection begins with matrix properties and the task.
- The mathematically valid method may not be the numerically best implementation.
Source links:
-
- Mathematics for Machine Learning book site
65. Q: What are the principal machine-learning applications connected to the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter connects matrix characteristics and decompositions with probability, dimensionality reduction, image processing, data compression, spectral methods, covariance calculations, network analysis, and latent-pattern discovery. Eigenvalue methods underpin PCA, Fisher discriminant analysis, multidimensional scaling, Isomap, Laplacian eigenmaps, Hessian eigenmaps, and spectral clustering. SVD supports low-rank approximation, preprocessing, compression, and interpretation of matrix-form data. The applications use the mathematical structure but require their own modeling assumptions and validation.
