Table 10
Principal Component Analysis: Dimensionality Reduction, Reconstruction, and Probabilistic Extensions
1. Q: What is the central purpose of principal component analysis (PCA)?
A: Show answer
Principal component analysis is a linear dimensionality-reduction method that represents high-dimensional observations with a smaller number of coordinates while retaining as much of their variation as possible. It identifies an orthogonal subspace whose basis directions capture the largest variances in the data. Each observation is mapped to a shorter code in that subspace and can be reconstructed approximately in the original feature space. PCA is useful for compression, visualization, exploratory pattern analysis, and identifying dominant linear structure. It does not decide which information is important for a particular business or prediction objective; it optimizes a variance or reconstruction criterion defined from the data.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between reducing the number of coordinates and deciding which information matters for a downstream task. Give one example of each."
Key point:
- PCA finds a lower-dimensional linear representation that preserves high variance.
- The meaning of retained variance depends on the data and the purpose of the analysis.
2. Q: Why can high-dimensional data be difficult to analyze directly?
A: Show answer
A high-dimensional observation has many coordinates, which can make storage, computation, visualization, and interpretation difficult. Images provide a simple example: a 640 by 480 colour image has hundreds of thousands of pixels and multiple colour channels, so it can be represented by a very large vector. Many coordinates may be redundant or strongly correlated, meaning that the data occupy a much smaller region than the ambient feature space suggests. PCA exploits such linear structure by representing observations with fewer coordinates. Dimensionality reduction can make patterns easier to inspect and calculations more manageable, but the compressed representation should be checked for information loss.
Study instruction:
- "Take a high-dimensional example such as an image, document vector, or sensor record and identify why its original coordinates may be redundant or hard to interpret."
Key point:
- The number of recorded features can exceed the number of independent directions needed to describe the data.
- A compact representation can reduce storage and improve visualization, subject to reconstruction loss.
3. Q: What does it mean for high-dimensional data to have a lower intrinsic dimensionality?
A: Show answer
Intrinsic dimensionality refers to the number of independent directions needed to describe the meaningful variation in observations, which can be much smaller than the number of measured features. For example, points may be recorded with two coordinates while varying mostly along one line. The data still live in a two-dimensional ambient space, but a one-dimensional coordinate can describe them with little loss. Correlation and redundancy among features often create this situation. PCA searches for a linear subspace that captures much of the observed variation; it approximates this structure rather than proving that the data lie exactly on a lower-dimensional manifold.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch data in a two-dimensional plane that vary mostly along one direction, then explain the difference between ambient and intrinsic dimension."
Key point:
- Ambient dimension counts recorded coordinates; intrinsic dimension reflects the smaller structure needed to describe variation.
- PCA approximates low-dimensional linear structure and may still lose information.
4. Q: What is a principal component, and how is it related to the data covariance matrix?
A: Show answer
A principal component is a direction in feature space along which the projected data have a particular variance. In PCA, the directions are eigenvectors of the data covariance matrix, and their associated eigenvalues quantify the variance captured along those directions. The first principal component corresponds to the largest eigenvalue, the second to the next largest, and so on, with orthogonal directions for a symmetric covariance matrix. The component is a direction, not an original feature or an individual observation. A projected observation receives a coordinate, or score, along each selected component.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish a principal direction, its eigenvalue, and an observation's score on that direction."
Key point:
- Principal directions are covariance-matrix eigenvectors.
- The associated eigenvalue measures the variance captured along that direction.
5. Q: Why does the chapter assume centered data when deriving PCA?
A: Show answer
Centering subtracts the sample mean from each observation so that the transformed dataset has mean zero. This allows the covariance matrix to be written as S = (1/N)XXᵀ when observations are stored as columns of X, and it keeps the derivation focused on variation around the mean rather than on the location of the dataset. The chapter notes that the variance of a projected code is unaffected by a constant mean shift, so centering can be assumed without changing the relevant variance objective. In practical use, the mean must be estimated from the training data and retained for transforming later observations and reconstructing them.
Study instruction:
- "Write down the sample mean for a small dataset and show how subtracting it changes each observation while leaving pairwise differences unchanged."
Key point:
- PCA analyzes variation around the data mean.
- Store the training mean because the same centering transformation is needed for later data.
6. Q: How are the dataset matrix and covariance matrix defined in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter arranges N observations x₁ through x_N as the columns of a matrix X with dimensions D by N, where D is the number of features. For centered data, the covariance matrix is S = (1/N)XXᵀ = (1/N)Σ x_n x_nᵀ and has dimensions D by D. This convention differs from the common layout in which observations are rows, so dimensions must be checked before multiplying matrices. The covariance matrix is symmetric and describes variances along individual directions as well as covariances between pairs of features.
Study instruction:
- "For a dataset with D = 3 features and N = 5 observations, write the dimensions of X, Xᵀ, and S and verify that the matrix products are valid."
Key point:
- In this chapter, X is D by N because observations are columns.
- The centered-data covariance matrix S is D by D and equals XXᵀ divided by N.
7. Q: What do the projection matrix B and the latent code z represent in PCA?
A: Show answer
For a target dimension M smaller than D, the chapter defines B as a D by M matrix whose columns are the selected orthonormal basis vectors of the principal subspace. The low-dimensional code is z = Bᵀx, which contains M coordinates describing the observation relative to that basis. The reconstructed or projected vector is x̃ = Bz = BBᵀx and is expressed in the original D-dimensional feature space. Thus z is the compact representation, while x̃ is the approximation in the original coordinate system. The columns of B determine which subspace is retained.
Study instruction:
- "Label the dimensions of x, B, z, and x̃ in the equations z = Bᵀx and x̃ = Bz."
Key point:
- B contains the selected basis directions; z contains the compressed coordinates.
- The reconstructed vector x̃ is D-dimensional even though its code has only M coordinates.
8. Q: Why must the columns of the PCA projection matrix be orthonormal?
A: Show answer
Orthonormal columns have unit length and are mutually perpendicular, so BᵀB = I. This makes Bᵀx the coordinates of the orthogonal projection of x onto the subspace spanned by B, and it gives the projection matrix BBᵀ. Orthonormality also makes the variance and reconstruction derivations tractable because cross terms between distinct basis directions vanish. If the columns were not orthonormal, the projection onto their span would require the appropriate inverse Gram matrix rather than the simplified expression BBᵀ. The normalization also prevents an optimization from increasing projected variance merely by scaling a direction.
Study instruction:
- "Check BᵀB for a small matrix with two columns and explain what a nonzero off-diagonal entry would mean."
Key point:
- Orthonormality means unit-length, mutually perpendicular basis vectors.
- It supports the simple projection formula BBᵀx and prevents arbitrary scale inflation.
9. Q: What is the maximum-variance interpretation of PCA?
A: Show answer
The maximum-variance perspective chooses a lower-dimensional subspace so that the projected observations retain as much variance as possible. For a single unit direction b, the variance of the projected scalar bᵀx is bᵀSb, where S is the centered-data covariance matrix. The unit-length constraint is essential because without it the objective could be made arbitrarily large by multiplying b by a large constant. For M dimensions, PCA selects the M orthonormal directions that jointly capture the largest available variance. The resulting coordinates summarize the dominant linear variation in the dataset.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why maximizing projected variance needs a unit-length constraint and identify what the objective measures."
Key point:
- PCA chooses directions that maximize variance in the retained subspace.
- The variance criterion must be constrained so direction length cannot inflate the result.
10. Q: How does the constrained optimization for the first principal component lead to an eigenvector?
A: Show answer
For the first component, the chapter maximizes bᵀSb subject to bᵀb = 1. Introducing a Lagrange multiplier produces a Lagrangian of the form bᵀSb + λ(1 − bᵀb). Setting its derivative with respect to b to zero yields Sb = λb, together with the unit-norm condition. This is the eigenvalue equation for the covariance matrix. Substituting the eigenvector condition into the objective shows that the projected variance equals its eigenvalue. Therefore, the optimal direction is an eigenvector associated with the largest eigenvalue.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the Lagrangian argument on paper and explain why the multiplier becomes the projected variance."
Key point:
- The stationarity condition is Sb = λb.
- The largest covariance eigenvalue gives the maximum variance attainable by a unit direction.
11. Q: How should PCA eigenvalues and eigenvectors be interpreted together?
A: Show answer
An eigenvector supplies a direction in feature space, while its eigenvalue measures the variance of the data projected onto that direction. Eigenvectors of the symmetric covariance matrix can be chosen orthonormally, so they define perpendicular directions. Sorting eigenvalues from largest to smallest orders components by the amount of variance they capture. A large eigenvalue indicates substantial variation along its component; a small eigenvalue indicates little variation. The eigenvalue does not identify the cause or usefulness of the variation, so interpreting a component also requires examining its feature loadings and the context of the dataset.
Study instruction:
- "Sort a small set of eigenvalue-eigenvector pairs and state which directions should be retained first and why."
Key point:
- Eigenvectors define component directions; eigenvalues quantify projected variance.
- A high-variance direction is not automatically the most useful direction for every later task.
12. Q: Why are principal components selected in descending order of eigenvalues?
A: Show answer
Selecting eigenvectors associated with the largest eigenvalues ensures that the retained subspace captures the maximum possible variance for a fixed dimension M. The first component captures the greatest variance available in any unit direction. The second captures the greatest remaining variance subject to orthogonality to the first, and the process continues for later components. This ordering produces the optimal M-dimensional principal subspace under the chapter's variance criterion. Selecting directions with smaller eigenvalues while omitting larger ones would retain less total variance for the same number of coordinates.
Study instruction:
- "Given a list of eigenvalues, calculate the variance retained by the first M components and compare it with a different selection of M directions."
Key point:
- The M largest eigenvalues define the maximum-variance M-dimensional subspace.
- The ordering is optimal for the PCA variance objective, not necessarily for every predictive objective.
13. Q: How are later principal components found after the first component?
A: Show answer
After selecting the first components, the chapter considers the data remaining after their projected effects are removed. This residual data has a covariance matrix whose eigenvectors align with those of the original covariance matrix, while already selected directions have zero residual variance. The next component is the residual direction with the largest remaining eigenvalue. This gives a sequential interpretation: each component captures the strongest remaining variation while staying orthogonal to the earlier components. In the final solution, the selected directions are the leading eigenvectors of the original covariance matrix.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how removing the first component changes the remaining variance and why the next direction must be orthogonal to it."
Key point:
- Each new component captures the strongest variation not already represented.
- The selected principal directions are mutually orthogonal eigenvectors of the covariance matrix.
14. Q: What is explained variance, and how is the proportion of variance retained calculated?
A: Show answer
The variance captured by the first M components is the sum of their eigenvalues, VM = Σ from m = 1 to M of λ_m. The total variance is the sum of all D eigenvalues. The relative variance captured is therefore VM divided by the total variance, while the relative variance lost is one minus that proportion. These quantities help compare candidate dimensions and describe the degree of compression. They do not by themselves establish that a chosen representation preserves every feature relevant to a particular prediction or decision.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the cumulative explained-variance ratio for a hypothetical descending eigenvalue list and explain how it changes as M increases."
Key point:
- Explained variance accumulates by summing the selected eigenvalues.
- The retained proportion is a useful summary of compression, not a guarantee of task relevance.
15. Q: What is the reconstruction-error interpretation of PCA?
A: Show answer
The projection perspective asks for a low-dimensional subspace whose reconstructed observations are as close as possible to the originals. For each centered observation x_n, PCA forms x̃_n in the selected subspace and minimizes the average squared Euclidean distance between x_n and x̃_n. This quantity is the reconstruction error. It measures information lost by replacing each original vector with its lower-dimensional projection. The chapter shows that minimizing this error leads to the same principal subspace as maximizing retained variance, providing two complementary explanations of the same PCA solution.
Study instruction:
- "For a simple two-dimensional point and a chosen line, draw the original point, its projection, and the residual vector; identify the squared reconstruction error."
Key point:
- Reconstruction error measures the squared distance between each point and its projection.
- Minimizing average reconstruction error yields the same PCA subspace as maximizing variance.
16. Q: Why is the orthogonal projection the best reconstruction for a fixed subspace?
A: Show answer
Once a candidate subspace and its orthonormal basis are fixed, the chapter minimizes the squared distance between the original vector and a vector in that subspace. Differentiating the loss with respect to each code coordinate gives z_m = b_mᵀx. These coordinates produce x̃ = BBᵀx, the orthogonal projection. Geometrically, the residual x − x̃ is perpendicular to every direction in the subspace, so no other point in the same subspace is closer in Euclidean distance. The result separates two decisions: first choose the subspace, then use orthogonal projection to reconstruct each observation within it.
Study instruction:
- "For a fixed line or plane, compare the perpendicular projection with a non-perpendicular candidate and explain which has the shorter Euclidean distance."
Key point:
- The optimal code for a fixed orthonormal basis is z = Bᵀx.
- Orthogonal projection minimizes Euclidean reconstruction error within the chosen subspace.
17. Q: What does the projection matrix BBᵀ do?
A: Show answer
When B has orthonormal columns, BBᵀ maps an original vector x to its orthogonal projection x̃ in the selected principal subspace. The matrix is symmetric and idempotent, meaning (BBᵀ)ᵀ = BBᵀ and (BBᵀ)² = BBᵀ. Its rank is M, the dimension of the retained subspace. Applying it removes the components of x that lie in the orthogonal complement and retains the components along the selected principal directions. The compact code is still obtained separately as z = Bᵀx.
Study instruction:
- "Verify symmetry and idempotence for a simple orthonormal B and explain the geometric effect of multiplying by BBᵀ."
Key point:
- BBᵀ is the orthogonal projection operator onto the principal subspace.
- Its rank equals the number M of retained components.
18. Q: What is the orthogonal complement of the principal subspace?
A: Show answer
The orthogonal complement contains all directions perpendicular to the retained principal subspace. With a complete orthonormal basis b₁ through b_D, if the first M vectors form the principal subspace, the remaining D − M vectors span its orthogonal complement. The reconstruction residual x − x̃ lies entirely in this omitted subspace. It therefore represents the information removed by dimensionality reduction under the chosen basis. Thinking in terms of the complement makes the reconstruction-error formula transparent: the loss is the variation projected onto the directions that were not retained.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a retained subspace and its orthogonal complement, then show where the reconstruction residual lies."
Key point:
- The omitted directions form the orthogonal complement of the retained principal subspace.
- Reconstruction loss is the component of the data that lies in this omitted space.
19. Q: Why do maximum variance and minimum reconstruction error produce the same PCA solution?
A: Show answer
For centered data, the squared reconstruction error is the variance projected onto the orthogonal complement of the selected subspace. Minimizing the omitted variance is equivalent to maximizing the variance retained in the principal subspace because total variance is fixed. The chapter derives the reconstruction error as the sum of eigenvalues excluded from the first M components. Consequently, choosing the M largest eigenvalues both maximizes retained variance and minimizes average squared reconstruction error. These perspectives express the same optimization problem using different language: information retained versus distance lost.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the equivalence using the identity total variance = retained variance + omitted variance."
Key point:
- Total variance is fixed, so minimizing omitted variance maximizes retained variance.
- Both derivations select the eigenvectors associated with the M largest eigenvalues.
20. Q: How is the average squared reconstruction error related to the eigenvalues that are discarded?
A: Show answer
For projection onto the M-dimensional principal subspace, the chapter gives the average squared reconstruction error as the sum of the eigenvalues from M + 1 through D. Each discarded eigenvalue measures variance along a direction excluded from the reconstruction. Retaining more components reduces this tail sum, although the reduction may become small after the dominant components have been included. The formula provides a direct numerical link between the chosen dimension and the expected squared loss under the empirical covariance structure of the centered dataset.
Study instruction:
- "Take a descending eigenvalue list and compute the discarded-eigenvalue sum for several possible values of M."
Key point:
- The PCA reconstruction loss equals the sum of the discarded covariance eigenvalues.
- The tail sum makes the cost of compression explicit.
21. Q: How does the MNIST example illustrate the purpose of PCA?
A: Show answer
The chapter treats each 28 by 28 grayscale MNIST digit as a vector with 784 coordinates and uses a dataset containing 60,000 handwritten examples. A subset of 5,389 training images of the digit 8 is used to illustrate PCA reconstruction. The example shows that a relatively small number of principal components can retain much of the visual structure, while increasing the component count makes reconstructed digits progressively sharper. The example demonstrates how high-dimensional pixel data can be represented compactly, while also showing that compression quality depends on how many components are retained.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a 28 by 28 image becomes a 784-dimensional vector and describe what would be lost when only a few components are used."
Key point:
- An image can be treated as a vector whose coordinates are pixel values.
- The example shows a trade-off between a shorter code and reconstruction detail.
22. Q: What does the chapter's MNIST reconstruction comparison reveal about the number of principal components?
A: Show answer
The chapter compares reconstructions of digit images using 1, 10, 100, and 500 principal components. With one component, the reconstructed digit is recognizable only in a generic and blurry way; more components retain additional detail. With 500 components the reconstruction is close to the original, and the chapter reports that roughly 550 components can essentially reconstruct the training images of the digit 8. Using all 784 components would recover the image without compression loss in this setting. The illustration shows that gains are not necessarily uniform: early components can contribute much more than later ones.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the visual information that might survive at 1, 10, 100, and 500 components and relate the differences to the eigenvalue spectrum."
Key point:
- Adding components generally improves reconstruction detail.
- The marginal benefit of later components can decline as dominant variation is already captured.
23. Q: How can PCA be computed using an eigendecomposition of the covariance matrix?
A: Show answer
For centered observations stored as columns, form S = (1/N)XXᵀ and compute its eigenvalues and eigenvectors. Because S is symmetric, it has an orthonormal eigenbasis. Sort the eigenvalues in descending order, select the M associated eigenvectors, and place them as the columns of B. Each training or future observation can then be encoded by Bᵀx and reconstructed by BBᵀx. The direct approach is conceptually straightforward, but constructing and decomposing a very large D by D covariance matrix can be expensive when the feature dimension is high.
Study instruction:
- "Write the full computational sequence from centered X to S, sorted eigenpairs, B, and the projected code."
Key point:
- The leading covariance eigenvectors form the PCA projection matrix.
- Direct covariance eigendecomposition can be costly when D is very large.
24. Q: How is singular value decomposition related to PCA?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the singular value decomposition X = UΣVᵀ for the centered data matrix X. Since S = (1/N)XXᵀ, substituting the SVD gives S = (1/N)UΣΣᵀUᵀ. Thus the columns of U are eigenvectors of S, and the covariance eigenvalues are the squared singular values divided by N. SVD therefore provides another route to the principal directions without separately treating the covariance matrix as the primary object. The relationship is especially useful because numerical libraries provide efficient and stable SVD routines.
Study instruction:
- "Derive S = UΣΣᵀUᵀ/N from X = UΣVᵀ and identify which SVD factor contains the principal directions."
Key point:
- The left singular vectors of the centered data matrix are covariance eigenvectors.
- Each covariance eigenvalue equals the corresponding squared singular value divided by N.
25. Q: Why can truncated SVD be used as a low-rank approximation for PCA?
A: Show answer
PCA retains the leading M singular directions of the centered data matrix. Truncating the SVD to its first M singular values and corresponding left and right singular vectors creates a rank-M approximation X̃_M = U_MΣ_MV_Mᵀ. The Eckart–Young theorem states that this truncation is the best rank-M approximation to X under the spectral norm used in the chapter. This gives a direct matrix-approximation interpretation of PCA: it replaces a large data matrix with a lower-rank matrix that captures its dominant structure. The approximation does not mean every individual observation is reconstructed exactly.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what changes when the SVD is truncated after M singular values and why the resulting matrix has rank at most M."
Key point:
- Truncated SVD retains the leading singular directions and forms a rank-M approximation.
- The Eckart–Young theorem provides the best rank-M approximation under the stated spectral-norm criterion.
26. Q: What is the practical difference between computing a full eigendecomposition and computing only the leading principal components?
A: Show answer
A full eigendecomposition computes all eigenvalues and eigenvectors even when the application needs only a small number of leading directions. If only a few components are required, iterative methods that target the dominant eigenvectors can avoid much of this unnecessary work. The chapter describes power iteration as a simple approach for estimating the leading eigenvector. The appropriate method depends on matrix size, data shape, numerical requirements, and how many components are needed. In practice, numerical linear-algebra libraries provide algorithms for eigendecomposition and SVD rather than relying on symbolic roots of a characteristic polynomial.
Study instruction:
- "For a problem requiring only three components from a very large matrix, compare the purpose of a full decomposition with an iterative leading-component method."
Key point:
- Computing unused eigenvectors can waste time and memory.
- Iterative algorithms can target only the leading directions when that is all the task requires.
27. Q: Why does the chapter caution against solving large eigenvalue problems by finding roots of a characteristic polynomial?
A: Show answer
The characteristic-polynomial approach is a theoretical route to eigenvalues, but polynomial degree grows with matrix dimension. The chapter notes that general algebraic solutions are not available for polynomials of degree five or higher, as expressed by the Abel–Ruffini theorem. Even where a symbolic formulation can be written, it is not a practical numerical strategy for large data matrices. Modern linear-algebra packages instead use numerical algorithms for eigenvalues and singular values. The practical lesson is to understand the mathematical definition while relying on tested numerical routines for realistic problem sizes.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why writing a characteristic polynomial is not the same as having a practical algorithm for a large matrix."
Key point:
- The characteristic polynomial does not provide a general algebraic solution for large matrix dimensions.
- Numerical eigensolvers and SVD routines are the practical tools for PCA.
28. Q: How does power iteration estimate the dominant eigenvector?
A: Show answer
Power iteration starts with a nonzero vector having a component in the direction of the dominant eigenvector. It repeatedly multiplies the current vector by the matrix S and normalizes the result: x_{k+1} = Sx_k divided by the norm of Sx_k. Repeated multiplication amplifies the component associated with the largest eigenvalue relative to components associated with smaller eigenvalues, so the normalized sequence converges toward the dominant eigenvector under the usual separation and initialization conditions. The method is attractive when only the leading direction is needed, but it does not directly provide the entire eigenbasis.
Study instruction:
- "Carry out two or three normalized matrix-vector iterations on a small symmetric matrix and observe how the direction changes."
Key point:
- Power iteration repeatedly applies S and normalizes the vector.
- It targets the eigenvector associated with the largest eigenvalue, subject to appropriate initialization and spectral conditions.
29. Q: Why is ordinary covariance eigendecomposition challenging when the feature dimension is extremely large?
A: Show answer
The covariance matrix has D rows and D columns, so its storage grows quadratically with the number of features. Its eigendecomposition can also be computationally expensive, with the chapter describing cubic scaling in D as the relevant concern. For image data or other very wide observations, forming and decomposing a large D by D matrix may be infeasible. The chapter addresses the special case in which the number of observations N is much smaller than D by using the smaller N by N matrix XᵀX. This changes the size of the eigenproblem while retaining the nonzero eigenvalues needed for PCA.
Study instruction:
- "Estimate the dimensions and approximate storage requirements of a D by D covariance matrix for a high-dimensional application."
Key point:
- The covariance matrix grows with the square of the feature dimension.
- When N is much smaller than D, an alternative N by N eigenproblem can be more efficient.
30. Q: How does the dual covariance-matrix approach help when N is much smaller than D?
A: Show answer
With centered data X of size D by N, the feature-space covariance matrix is XXᵀ/N and has size D by D. When N is much smaller than D, the chapter considers the smaller Gram matrix XᵀX/N, which has size N by N. The nonzero eigenvalues of XXᵀ and XᵀX are the same, so the smaller matrix contains the nonzero spectrum needed to recover principal directions. Once its eigenvectors are found, multiplication by X maps them back to feature space; the resulting vectors must be normalized. The method avoids a much larger covariance eigendecomposition.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the dimensions of XXᵀ and XᵀX for D = 20,000 and N = 500 and explain which matrix is smaller."
Key point:
- The dual matrix XᵀX is N by N rather than D by D.
- Its nonzero eigenvalues match those of XXᵀ, enabling recovery of feature-space directions.
31. Q: How are the feature-space eigenvectors recovered from the smaller matrix XᵀX?
A: Show answer
The chapter defines c_m = Xᵀb_m, where b_m is an eigenvector of XXᵀ/N. Multiplying the original eigenvalue equation by Xᵀ shows that c_m is an eigenvector of XᵀX/N with the same nonzero eigenvalue. Conversely, if c_m is an eigenvector of XᵀX/N, multiplication by X produces a vector in feature space proportional to the corresponding eigenvector of XXᵀ/N. The recovered vector Xc_m must be normalized so that it meets the unit-length requirement of the PCA basis. The approach relies on nonzero eigenvalues and appropriate rank conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the mapping from c_m in observation space to Xc_m in feature space and explain why normalization is required."
Key point:
- Multiplication by X maps a Gram-matrix eigenvector into feature space.
- Normalize the recovered vector before using it as a PCA component.
32. Q: What happens to the rank and eigenvalues of the covariance matrix when N is smaller than D?
A: Show answer
For centered observations arranged as columns, the rank of S = XXᵀ/N cannot exceed the rank of X, which is at most N. In the chapter's case with N much smaller than D and no duplicate data points, the covariance matrix has rank N and therefore many zero eigenvalues in the D-dimensional space. These zero directions indicate that the sample covariance contains no variation outside the span of the observed data vectors. This structure explains why the smaller N by N matrix can be used to compute the nonzero eigenvalues more efficiently.
Study instruction:
- "Use rank(X) ≤ min(D,N) to explain why a D by D covariance matrix may have a large null space when N is small."
Key point:
- The rank of the sample covariance cannot exceed the number of observations.
- When N is much smaller than D, many feature-space eigenvalues may be zero.
33. Q: What sequence of steps does the chapter recommend for practical PCA?
A: Show answer
The chapter's workflow begins by computing the training-data mean and subtracting it from each observation. It may then standardize each feature by dividing by its standard deviation, compute the covariance matrix, and obtain eigenvalues and eigenvectors. The eigenvectors are ordered by descending eigenvalue, the first M are assembled into B, and observations are projected into the principal subspace. When reconstruction is required, the projected data are mapped back to the original feature coordinates and any scaling transformation is reversed. The sequence should be documented so that later observations are processed consistently.
Study instruction:
- "Write a numbered PCA workflow for a real dataset and annotate where the mean, feature scales, eigenpairs, and projection matrix are stored."
Key point:
- The workflow is preprocess, estimate covariance, decompose, select components, and project.
- Keep preprocessing parameters with the fitted PCA model for consistent later use.
34. Q: What is the purpose of mean subtraction in practical PCA?
A: Show answer
Mean subtraction translates the dataset so that its feature-wise mean is zero. It is the practical counterpart of the centered-data assumption used throughout the derivations and makes the covariance matrix represent variation around the sample mean. The chapter notes that centering can also reduce the risk of numerical problems. It is not the same as dividing features by their standard deviations: centering changes location, while scaling changes units and relative feature magnitudes. The mean estimated from training data should be applied unchanged to validation, test, and future observations.
Study instruction:
- "Use a small numeric dataset to compute the feature means and construct the centered matrix by subtracting the mean from every observation."
Key point:
- Centering subtracts the training mean from each feature.
- Centering and standardization are distinct operations with different effects.
35. Q: When does standardization change the PCA analysis?
A: Show answer
Standardization divides each feature by its standard deviation after centering, so the resulting coordinates are unit-free and have variance one along each feature axis in the training sample. This changes the relative weighting of features: a variable originally measured on a large numerical scale no longer dominates merely because of its units. The chapter presents standardization as a practical step, but it is not universally neutral. Whether to standardize depends on the meaning of feature scales and the analytical question. If the scale differences are meaningful, removing them may change the structure that PCA should capture.
Study instruction:
- "Compare PCA on a dataset with features measured in dollars and millimetres before and after standardization, and explain which scale choice matches the intended analysis."
Key point:
- Standardization rescales each feature using its training standard deviation.
- Use it when unit and scale differences should not dominate the variance criterion.
36. Q: Why must a new observation be standardized using the training-set mean and standard deviation?
A: Show answer
The PCA basis is learned from the transformed training data, so a new observation must be expressed in the same coordinate system. The chapter's projection step applies the training mean and standard deviation to each feature before multiplying by Bᵀ. Recomputing the mean or scale from an individual new observation or from a changing test batch would alter the coordinate system and make its scores inconsistent with the fitted basis. The stored preprocessing parameters are therefore part of the fitted PCA transformation, not optional descriptive statistics.
Study instruction:
- "Create a checklist of preprocessing parameters that must be saved with a fitted PCA model and show how they transform one new sample."
Key point:
- Use the training transformation for every later observation.
- Re-estimating scales on the test sample changes the coordinate system and invalidates consistent comparison.
37. Q: How are new observations projected into the PCA space?
A: Show answer
After applying the fitted centering and, where used, standardization, a new observation x is projected by calculating z = Bᵀx. This produces M coordinates rather than D original feature values. If a reconstructed approximation is needed in the transformed feature space, compute x̃ = Bz = BBᵀx. The chapter emphasizes that z is the low-dimensional code and x̃ is the projected vector in the original-dimensional coordinate space. A projection is not the same as the exact original observation unless the retained subspace spans all relevant dimensions.
Study instruction:
- "Take one new observation and calculate its code z and its reconstructed projection x̃ using a supplied orthonormal B."
Key point:
- The PCA score vector is z = Bᵀx after training preprocessing.
- Reconstruction uses x̃ = Bz in the transformed feature space.
38. Q: How is a PCA reconstruction returned to the original feature scale?
A: Show answer
If standardization was applied, the projection is first computed in standardized coordinates. To return to the original scale, multiply each reconstructed feature by its training standard deviation and then add its training mean. The chapter gives this inverse transformation explicitly. Omitting the inverse scaling leaves values in standardized units, while adding the mean without reversing the scale also gives incorrect original-unit values. The same saved parameters used for preprocessing must therefore be used in reverse order for reconstruction and interpretation.
Study instruction:
- "For one feature, standardize a value, project it, and reverse the scaling by multiplying by the stored standard deviation and adding the stored mean."
Key point:
- Undo standardization by multiplying by the stored standard deviation and adding the stored mean.
- The inverse transform must use the same training parameters as the forward transform.
39. Q: How should the number of principal components M be selected?
A: Show answer
The number M controls the trade-off between compression and retained information. The chapter provides measures such as cumulative explained variance and the discarded-eigenvalue reconstruction error, and its MNIST example illustrates the diminishing gains from adding later components. It also discusses using nested cross-validation or Bayesian model-selection criteria to estimate intrinsic dimensionality. A threshold for retained variance can be a useful practical rule, but it is not a universal guarantee. The choice should consider reconstruction needs, computational constraints, downstream evaluation, and whether the representation remains interpretable.
Study instruction:
- "Choose candidate values of M using cumulative variance and reconstruction error, then state what additional validation would be needed for a task-specific use."
Key point:
- M determines the amount of compression and the remaining reconstruction loss.
- Use validation or model-selection criteria when the goal extends beyond descriptive variance retention.
40. Q: What is probabilistic PCA (PPCA), and how does it extend ordinary PCA?
A: Show answer
Probabilistic PCA expresses dimensionality reduction as a latent-variable generative model. Instead of describing a projection only through a deterministic variance or reconstruction objective, it assumes a lower-dimensional random variable generates the observed data through a linear mapping plus Gaussian noise. This supplies a likelihood, a posterior distribution over latent coordinates, and a way to reason about uncertainty. The chapter shows that ordinary PCA is recovered as a special noise-free limit of PPCA's maximum-likelihood solution. The probabilistic formulation also connects PCA to related latent-variable methods.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast the deterministic PCA mapping with the PPCA generative assumption and list what the probabilistic formulation adds."
Key point:
- PPCA models observations as generated from lower-dimensional latent variables plus noise.
- Ordinary PCA appears as a special limiting case of the probabilistic model.
41. Q: What is the generative process assumed by probabilistic PCA?
A: Show answer
PPCA assumes a latent vector z in M dimensions with a standard-normal prior and generates an observed vector x in D dimensions through x = Bz + μ + ε. The noise ε is Gaussian with zero mean and covariance σ²I. Conditional on z and the model parameters, x is therefore Gaussian with mean Bz + μ and isotropic covariance σ²I. To generate an observation, first sample z from its prior and then sample x from the conditional distribution. This model treats the latent variable as a lower-dimensional cause of the observed features.
Study instruction:
- "Write the two sampling steps for PPCA and identify which quantities are random and which are shared model parameters."
Key point:
- The prior is z ~ N(0,I); the observation is x = Bz + μ + Gaussian noise.
- B, μ, and σ² describe the shared mapping and observation-noise model.
42. Q: Why does PPCA integrate out the latent variable when forming the likelihood?
A: Show answer
For parameter estimation, the likelihood must be a function of the observed data and model parameters rather than an unobserved latent variable. PPCA therefore integrates p(x|z,B,μ,σ²) against the prior p(z). The resulting marginal distribution is Gaussian with mean μ and covariance BBᵀ + σ²I. This marginal likelihood can be used for maximum-likelihood or maximum-a-posteriori estimation of the model parameters. Using only the conditional distribution would leave z in the expression and would not provide the marginal likelihood required for parameter estimation.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the distinction between a conditional density p(x|z,parameters) and a marginal likelihood p(x|parameters)."
Key point:
- The likelihood for observed data marginalizes over the unobserved latent variable.
- The PPCA marginal covariance is BBᵀ + σ²I.
43. Q: What is the posterior distribution of the latent code in PPCA?
A: Show answer
Given an observed vector x, PPCA uses Gaussian conditioning to obtain a posterior p(z|x) that is Gaussian. Its mean is Bᵀ(BBᵀ + σ²I)⁻¹(x − μ), and its covariance is I − Bᵀ(BBᵀ + σ²I)⁻¹B. The posterior mean provides an estimate of the latent coordinates after taking the noise model into account, while the covariance describes uncertainty about those coordinates. Unlike the deterministic score Bᵀx of ordinary PCA, the PPCA posterior represents uncertainty rather than only a single projected value.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the role of the posterior mean and covariance in the PPCA formula and explain why both are needed."
Key point:
- The posterior mean estimates latent coordinates conditional on the observation.
- The posterior covariance quantifies uncertainty in those coordinates.
44. Q: Why does the PPCA posterior covariance not depend on the observed value x?
A: Show answer
Under the linear-Gaussian PPCA model, the conditional covariance obtained by Gaussian conditioning depends on the model covariance matrices and parameters, not on the particular observed vector. The chapter's posterior covariance C = I − Bᵀ(BBᵀ + σ²I)⁻¹B is therefore the same for observations evaluated under the same fitted model. The posterior mean does depend on x, so different observations can occupy different positions in latent space while having the same conditional uncertainty structure. This is a property of this Gaussian model and should not be generalized to every probabilistic latent-variable model.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the observation-dependent and observation-independent terms in the PPCA posterior equations."
Key point:
- For PPCA, the posterior mean changes with x but the stated posterior covariance does not.
- The result follows from the linear-Gaussian conditioning structure.
45. Q: How can posterior uncertainty in PPCA help assess a new observation?
A: Show answer
The posterior covariance describes the spread of plausible latent coordinates given an observation. The chapter notes that a covariance with a small determinant corresponds to a relatively small uncertainty volume in latent space, while a posterior with substantial variance can indicate that the embedding is uncertain and may warrant further examination. The model can also be used to sample latent values from the posterior and generate corresponding observations, allowing plausible reconstructions to be explored. High posterior uncertainty is a diagnostic signal rather than proof that an observation is an outlier; it should be assessed alongside the fitted model and the observation's context.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what a broad versus concentrated posterior over z means and why uncertainty alone cannot prove that a sample is anomalous."
Key point:
- Posterior covariance measures uncertainty about latent coordinates.
- Large uncertainty can prompt investigation but is not by itself a definitive outlier label.
46. Q: In what sense does ordinary PCA arise from PPCA in the noise-free limit?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows that when the PPCA observation-noise variance approaches zero, the covariance of the observation becomes BBᵀ. The maximum-likelihood solution then aligns with the eigendecomposition of the data covariance matrix, using the leading eigenvectors and their associated eigenvalues. In that limit, the probabilistic model's retained subspace corresponds to the ordinary PCA subspace. This connection explains why the deterministic maximum-variance and minimum-reconstruction-error derivations are consistent with the latent-variable formulation, while PPCA also offers a way to model noise when it is not negligible.
Study instruction:
- "Describe what happens to the PPCA observation covariance as σ approaches zero and connect the resulting factorization to PCA."
Key point:
- As observation noise tends to zero, PPCA recovers the ordinary PCA covariance structure.
- PCA can be understood as the noise-free special case of PPCA under the chapter's solution.
47. Q: What does Bayesian PCA add beyond maximum-likelihood PCA?
A: Show answer
Bayesian PCA places prior distributions on model parameters such as μ, B, and σ² and integrates over parameter uncertainty rather than relying only on point estimates. The chapter explains that this can help address overfitting and can support automatic selection of latent dimensionality. The required integration is analytically difficult, so approximate inference methods such as Markov chain Monte Carlo or variational inference may be used. This extension changes the treatment of uncertainty: uncertainty in the model parameters is represented, not just uncertainty about latent coordinates conditional on fixed parameters.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a point estimate of B with a Bayesian treatment that represents a distribution over B and explain the potential benefit."
Key point:
- Bayesian PCA represents uncertainty over model parameters through prior distributions.
- Approximate inference is needed when exact marginalization is analytically intractable.
48. Q: How does factor analysis differ from probabilistic PCA?
A: Show answer
Both methods use latent variables and a linear relationship between latent and observed variables, but the chapter distinguishes their noise assumptions. PPCA uses the same isotropic observation-noise variance σ² in every observed dimension, whereas factor analysis allows a separate variance σ_d² for each dimension. This gives factor analysis a more flexible likelihood, but the chapter notes that it no longer has the same closed-form maximum-likelihood solution and typically requires an iterative method such as expectation maximization. It also notes differences in invariance and optimization properties, so the methods should not be treated as interchangeable.
Study instruction:
- "Build a comparison table in your notes showing the noise covariance assumptions and estimation implications of PPCA and factor analysis."
Key point:
- PPCA assumes isotropic noise; factor analysis allows dimension-specific noise variances.
- Greater likelihood flexibility changes the estimation problem and its guarantees.
49. Q: What is independent component analysis (ICA), and how is it related to PCA?
A: Show answer
Independent component analysis is a related latent-variable approach that changes the prior on the latent sources from the Gaussian form used in PPCA to non-Gaussian distributions. The chapter illustrates ICA with blind-source separation: several speakers' voices are mixed in microphones, and the goal is to recover the constituent signals. PCA can identify a useful lower-dimensional subspace but is invariant to rotations within that subspace, so it does not by itself identify the original independent sources. ICA adds assumptions about non-Gaussian latent variables to resolve a different problem from variance-maximizing PCA.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why finding a low-dimensional subspace is not the same as separating individual latent sources in a mixture."
Key point:
- PCA finds principal directions and a subspace based on covariance.
- ICA uses non-Gaussian latent-source assumptions for tasks such as blind-source separation.
50. Q: What is the kernel trick in the context of dimensionality reduction?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the kernel trick as a way to compute inner products between feature representations that may belong to an extremely high- or infinite-dimensional space without explicitly constructing those feature vectors. Kernel PCA uses this idea to extend PCA beyond a direct linear projection in the original input coordinates. The method relies on inner products encoded by a kernel function, making it possible to capture certain nonlinear structures through an implicit feature mapping. The chapter presents it as an extension related to the high-dimensional and latent-variable perspectives rather than as the same operation as ordinary linear PCA.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between explicitly constructing a high-dimensional feature vector and computing its inner products through a kernel."
Key point:
- Kernel methods use implicit feature-space inner products.
- Kernel PCA extends the PCA idea to nonlinear structure through an implicit mapping.
51. Q: How can PCA be interpreted as a linear autoencoder?
A: Show answer
PCA can be represented as a two-stage linear network. The encoder maps x to z = Bᵀx, compressing the D-dimensional observation to M coordinates. The decoder maps z back to x̃ = Bz, producing the orthogonal projection onto the principal subspace. If the objective minimizes the average squared reconstruction error and the linear mappings use the PCA solution, the result matches the projection formulation already derived in the chapter. This interpretation connects classical matrix methods with a familiar machine-learning architecture, while retaining the specific orthogonality and optimality assumptions of PCA.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the encoder and decoder as separate linear maps and label the dimensions at each stage."
Key point:
- The PCA encoder is Bᵀ and the decoder is B.
- The linear autoencoder reconstructs the orthogonal projection onto the principal subspace.
52. Q: What distinguishes a deep autoencoder from the linear autoencoder interpretation of PCA?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that replacing the linear encoder and decoder with multilayer neural networks creates a nonlinear or deep autoencoder. Such networks can model nonlinear mappings, whereas ordinary PCA is restricted to a linear subspace. The chapter notes that if the neural network activation functions are identities, the deep architecture reduces to linear mappings and can become equivalent to PCA under the corresponding setup. Deep autoencoders therefore generalize the compression-and-reconstruction idea, but their optimization and representation properties are not the same as the closed-form linear PCA solution.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the representational capacity of a linear PCA encoder-decoder with a nonlinear neural encoder-decoder."
Key point:
- PCA is a linear special case within the broader autoencoder idea.
- Nonlinear layers can represent structures that a single linear subspace cannot.
53. Q: How does the information-theoretic interpretation of PCA describe compression?
A: Show answer
The chapter views the low-dimensional code as a compressed representation of the original observation. Reconstructing from a shorter code generally introduces distortion, so the compression is lossy. It connects the goal of retaining information with correlation and mutual information between the original data and the code. Under the chapter's discussion, maximizing mutual information leads to the same PCA solution in the corresponding setting. This interpretation adds an information-theoretic perspective to the two earlier views based on variance and squared reconstruction error.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why dimensionality reduction is usually lossy and how mutual information offers another way to discuss retained information."
Key point:
- The latent code is shorter than the original data and may not preserve every detail.
- The chapter links PCA's solution to a mutual-information interpretation of compression.
54. Q: What is the role of the latent variable in PPCA as a generative explanation?
A: Show answer
The latent variable z is a lower-dimensional cause from which the observed vector x is generated through a linear transformation, an offset μ, and noise. This direction of the generative model matters: PPCA specifies how latent coordinates produce observations, while inference works in the reverse direction by calculating p(z|x). Once a latent coordinate is sampled, the decoder-like mapping can generate an observation that resembles the training data when the latent value is in a plausible region. The chapter's MNIST illustration shows that different points in a two-dimensional latent space can generate different digit shapes, rotations, and sizes.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the generative direction from z to x and then explain how inference obtains information about z after observing x."
Key point:
- PPCA treats the lower-dimensional latent variable as a generative cause of the observation.
- Inference reverses the direction probabilistically by computing p(z|x).
55. Q: Why can generated observations look unrealistic when sampled far from the training data in latent space?
A: Show answer
The chapter's MNIST example shows that latent points close to observed training codes tend to generate more plausible digits, while points farther away can produce artifacts. The model has learned its structure from the training distribution, so an arbitrary latent coordinate is not guaranteed to correspond to a well-supported observation. Sampling the latent space is therefore useful for exploring the learned representation, but the results should be judged against the distribution and examples used to fit the model. The example illustrates both the generative capability and the limitations of extrapolating beyond familiar latent regions.
Study instruction:
- "Compare generated examples near a cluster of training codes with examples far from that cluster and identify visible artifacts."
Key point:
- The learned model is best supported in latent regions represented by training data.
- A generative mapping does not guarantee realistic outputs for every latent coordinate.
56. Q: How can PPCA be used to generate plausible reconstructions from a new observation?
A: Show answer
For a new observation x, first calculate the posterior distribution p(z|x) using the fitted PPCA model. Then sample a latent coordinate from that posterior and sample a reconstructed observation from p(x|z,B,μ,σ²). Repeating these steps yields a collection of plausible reconstructions rather than only one fixed estimate. The spread of results reflects uncertainty in the latent representation and observation model. This is different from simply computing the deterministic PCA projection, because the probabilistic procedure explicitly represents uncertainty and observation noise.
Study instruction:
- "Write the two-stage posterior predictive sampling procedure and identify which distribution is used at each stage."
Key point:
- First sample latent coordinates from p(z|x), then sample observations conditional on z.
- Repeated draws explore reconstruction uncertainty under the PPCA model.
57. Q: How does the chapter suggest estimating the number of latent dimensions in a probabilistic setting?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses several approaches to selecting latent dimensionality. One heuristic from Gavish and Donoho compares singular values with a threshold based on estimated noise variance and matrix dimension. It also mentions nested cross-validation and Bayesian model-selection criteria as alternatives. Bayesian PCA can place priors on parameters and integrate over them, allowing model complexity to be assessed while accounting for uncertainty. No single rule is automatically appropriate for every dataset; the choice depends on the noise assumptions, sample size, model objective, and validation evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the noise-threshold heuristic, cross-validation, and Bayesian model selection as different approaches to choosing M."
Key point:
- Latent dimensionality can be assessed using spectral heuristics or model-selection procedures.
- The choice should reflect the noise model and the intended use of the representation.
58. Q: What is the purpose of the noise variance parameter σ² in PPCA?
A: Show answer
The PPCA noise parameter σ² describes isotropic observation noise added after the latent vector is mapped into feature space. It appears in the conditional covariance σ²I and in the marginal covariance BBᵀ + σ²I. This term prevents the model from treating every observed deviation as signal explained by the retained latent subspace. The chapter also relates the estimated noise variance to residual variance in the orthogonal complement of the principal subspace. Its value therefore influences the inferred latent representation and the model's uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Locate σ² in the conditional and marginal covariance formulas and explain what changes if it becomes very small."
Key point:
- σ² models isotropic residual observation noise in PPCA.
- It separates variation explained by latent factors from variation left as noise.
59. Q: Why is the PPCA maximum-likelihood solution not identical to an arbitrary choice of basis rotation?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows that the maximum-likelihood loading matrix in PPCA is not unique in its orientation within the retained latent space: it can be right-multiplied by an orthogonal rotation without changing the same represented covariance. This reflects the fact that the latent coordinates can be rotated while preserving the model's observable covariance structure. The principal subspace is identifiable in the relevant sense, but a particular coordinate orientation within that subspace need not be unique. This is consistent with the rotational ambiguity noted in the connection between covariance decompositions and latent-variable representations.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why rotating latent coordinates with an orthogonal matrix can preserve the represented subspace while changing the coordinate labels."
Key point:
- The latent basis orientation can be non-unique even when the represented subspace is unchanged.
- Interpret the subspace and covariance structure separately from arbitrary coordinate rotations.
60. Q: What does the chapter say about using expectation maximization in PCA-related models?
A: Show answer
Expectation maximization is mentioned as an iterative method for estimating parameters in models where closed-form maximum-likelihood solutions are not available or where data arrive sequentially. The chapter notes its use for factor analysis and for parameter estimation in probabilistic PCA settings such as streaming data. EM alternates between estimating latent-variable expectations under current parameters and updating parameters using those expectations. The chapter does not present a full EM derivation, so it should be treated here as an estimation approach associated with the probabilistic extensions rather than as the core closed-form computation of ordinary PCA.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the closed-form eigenvector computation of ordinary PCA from iterative parameter estimation in latent-variable extensions."
Key point:
- Ordinary PCA can be computed through matrix decompositions.
- EM is an iterative estimation method used in related probabilistic latent-variable settings.
61. Q: How should PCA be interpreted when the data contain correlated features?
A: Show answer
Correlated features indicate that some coordinates tend to vary together, so the data may occupy fewer dominant directions than the raw feature count suggests. PCA constructs orthogonal combinations of the original features that summarize these patterns of joint variation. A component can therefore combine many original variables rather than correspond to one feature. Examining its eigenvector coefficients, often called loadings, helps explain which variables contribute to the direction. Correlation is one reason PCA can compress data, but it does not by itself prove that the resulting components have a simple causal or semantic meaning.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a component vector with several nonzero coefficients and explain how it represents a joint pattern among original features."
Key point:
- PCA can combine correlated original features into orthogonal component directions.
- Component loadings describe associations with features, not causal explanations.
62. Q: What are the main limitations of PCA that should be considered before using it?
A: Show answer
PCA is linear, so a compact linear subspace may not capture nonlinear structure well. It ranks directions by variance, not by their usefulness for a particular supervised prediction, operational decision, or scientific question. It can also be sensitive to feature scaling, and the retained components may be difficult to interpret when they combine many variables. A lower-dimensional representation necessarily discards some variation unless all relevant directions are retained. The chapter's discussions of kernel PCA, deep autoencoders, and probabilistic latent-variable models illustrate possible extensions, but each introduces its own assumptions and computational trade-offs.
Study instruction:
- "For a proposed application, identify whether the desired structure is linear, whether feature scales are comparable, and whether variance is an appropriate importance criterion."
Key point:
- PCA optimizes a linear variance or reconstruction objective.
- Validate the representation against the actual analytical goal and data structure.
63. Q: How should an analyst decide between PCA, PPCA, factor analysis, and ICA?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents these methods as related but distinct tools. Ordinary PCA is suitable when the goal is a compact linear subspace based on variance or reconstruction error. PPCA adds a Gaussian latent-variable and isotropic-noise model, providing likelihoods and posterior uncertainty. Factor analysis allows different noise variances across observed dimensions and has a more flexible likelihood, often requiring iterative estimation. ICA changes the latent prior to non-Gaussian distributions to identify independent sources, such as in blind-source separation. Method choice should follow the question being asked, not simply the shared use of latent variables or matrix operations.
Study instruction:
- "Match four example objectives—compression, probabilistic embedding, dimension-specific noise modelling, and source separation—to the four methods."
Key point:
- PCA, PPCA, factor analysis, and ICA have different objectives and assumptions.
- Select the method according to the structure and inference problem the application requires.
64. Q: What is the difference between a principal component score and a reconstructed observation?
A: Show answer
A principal component score vector z contains the M coordinates of an observation in the retained basis. It is the compact representation used for downstream plotting, storage, or analysis in the reduced space. The reconstructed vector x̃ = Bz is D-dimensional and lies in the selected subspace of the original feature space. Although x̃ has the same number of coordinates as x, it generally differs from the original observation because the discarded orthogonal-complement directions are absent. Confusing the score with the reconstruction can lead to incorrect interpretation of dimensions and reported results.
Study instruction:
- "Label a numerical example with x, z, and x̃ and state the dimension and role of each quantity."
Key point:
- Scores are M-dimensional coordinates; reconstructions are D-dimensional projected vectors.
- The reconstruction is an approximation, not necessarily the original observation.
65. Q: How can the PCA workflow be made reproducible when applied to future datasets?
A: Show answer
A reproducible PCA workflow records the training-data mean, any feature standard deviations, the selected component matrix B, the component count M, and the conventions used for the data matrix and covariance calculation. Future observations must be transformed with the same saved centering and scaling values, then projected with the same B. If reconstruction is required, the inverse scaling must use those same stored parameters. The chapter's practical steps make clear that preprocessing and projection are one fitted transformation, so retaining only the eigenvectors is insufficient when centering or standardization has been used.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a model-artifact checklist that would allow another analyst to transform new data exactly as the original training workflow did."
Key point:
- Save preprocessing parameters and the learned projection matrix together.
- Apply the same transformations and component ordering to every later dataset.
66. Q: What is the integrated lesson of the chapter's treatment of dimensionality reduction with PCA?
A: Show answer
The chapter develops PCA through complementary views: maximizing projected variance, minimizing squared reconstruction error, computing leading covariance eigenvectors or singular vectors, and modelling low-dimensional structure probabilistically. It explains practical computation, including low-rank SVD approximations and the smaller Gram-matrix approach when observations are fewer than features. It then extends the core idea through PPCA, Bayesian PCA, factor analysis, ICA, kernel methods, and autoencoders. Across these topics, the central discipline is to understand the data geometry, preprocessing assumptions, retained dimension, noise model, and computational constraints before choosing an algorithm.
Study instruction:
- "Create a chapter map linking the variance, reconstruction, decomposition, high-dimensional, practical, and latent-variable sections, and add one use case for each."
Key point:
- PCA is a unified idea expressed through variance, reconstruction, and matrix decomposition.
- Extensions add different assumptions about noise, latent structure, nonlinearity, or source independence.
